#7156. Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit

LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the voice-driven era of computing. Our platform gives developers everything they need to build, test, deploy, scale, and observe agents in production. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice AI applications for OpenAI, xAI, Salesforce, Coursera, Spotify, and thousands of others, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

The Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit is the technical owner and architect of the systems that power our go-to-market engine. You'll decide how our GTM stack is designed, what we build versus buy, and where we invest next — and you'll be hands-on building it: Salesforce architecture, integrations, enrichment pipelines, and the agentic workflows that help Sales and Marketing move faster and smarter.

Salesforce is our system of record for revenue, pipeline, and customer data. HubSpot is our marketing layer for campaigns and lead generation, and what it captures needs to flow cleanly and reliably into Salesforce. Around them sits a growing stack of enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and data platform tools. You'll own the architecture that ties it all together: disciplined change management, observable pipelines, data models built for AI consumption, and agentic workflows that act on GTM signals at scale — designed to serve the business for years, not quarters.

This role sits at the intersection of GTM, Data, Finance, and Engineering. You'll work directly with senior leaders to shape the GTM systems roadmap, turn business strategy into technical plans, and guide decisions that span multiple teams. You'll also help grow the engineers around you, setting the technical standards and practices the team builds on.

You understand the difference between owning a system and gatekeeping one. Sales lives in Salesforce. Marketing lives in HubSpot. Your job is to make sure both are governed well enough that others can work in them effectively, and powerful enough that the GTM team can do things they couldn't do before.

You'll thrive as a Staff GTM Systems Engineer if you:

Are known as the go-to person for tough technical problems, and have good judgment about which ones matter most.

Think in systems and long time horizons, and can make confident architectural decisions with incomplete information.

Can clearly explain complex technical concepts and tradeoffs to engineers and executives alike.

Believe systems should be managed with engineering discipline.

Ship fast and reliably — your work holds up in production.

Are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools.

Find real satisfaction in mentoring other engineers and helping them grow.

Own the architecture of the GTM systems stack: define how Salesforce, HubSpot, enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and the data platform fit together, and write the technical strategy that guides how it evolves.

Lead build-vs-buy and procurement decisions: evaluate vendors, run proofs of concept, assess security and integration fit, and partner with Director of GTM Systems and Analytics, Finance, Legal, and IT on selection and renewals.

Shape the GTM systems roadmap with senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, RevOps, Finance, and Engineering: align on requirements, sequence investments, make clear tradeoffs, and lead cross-functional initiatives to completion.

Own Salesforce as the engineering system of record for revenue: data model, flows and automation, validation rules, field governance, and access control.

Own HubSpot for campaigns and lead generation, along with the HubSpot-Salesforce sync — so leads, contacts, and engagement signals reach the revenue pipeline without drift or silent failures.

Build and maintain integrations across the GTM stack, and partner with GTM Analytics Engineering on reliable data flows to the central data platform.

Design and deploy agentic workflows that consume GTM data, surface actionable signals, and automate manual operations — and set the patterns and guardrails others build on.

Establish observability and change management for critical GTM systems: alerts, dashboards, and runbooks; reviewed code changes, documented config, and rollback paths.

Mentor engineers across experience levels and set the technical standards for the team.

Partner with the Billing Systems Engineer to automate the CPQ-to-Billing handoff.

Extensive engineering experience in GTM or business systems, including owning a GTM stack's architecture end to end — build-vs-buy calls, vendor evaluations, and systems designed for a multi-year horizon.

Deep, hands-on Salesforce expertise — you understand the data model, have built complex flows and automation, and you think about it as an engineering system, not an admin tool. This is the primary bar.

HubSpot experience in a marketing context, including the HubSpot-Salesforce sync and what breaks and why.

Technical depth beyond the UI — you've built integrations with APIs, webhooks, and event streams, and you know when a native tool (a Flow, a Clay table, a HubSpot workflow) is the right answer versus when it's time to write code.

Agentic workflow experience — you've designed and deployed AI agents that act on GTM data in production.

Strong change management practices — you've shipped changes to live systems while protecting active deals and campaigns.

Experience influencing senior stakeholders — translating business strategy into system requirements and roadmaps.

A history of mentoring engineers, from early-career to senior.

Salesforce CPQ: approvals, order forms, discounting rules, and the closed-won-to-billing path.

SQL and data modeling fluency; familiarity with a warehouse-centric stack (BigQuery, Snowflake, dbt, reverse ETL).

Experience with billing platforms (Stripe, Zuora, Maxio) and the handoffs between GTM systems and billing.

Experience managing a GTM tooling budget or vendor portfolio.

Experience as a technical lead on a GTM or business systems team.

An opportunity to build something truly impactful to the world

Contribute to open source alongside world-class engineers

#7151. Senior Manager, Business Operations at Clutch

Clutch is at a pivotal moment. We're growing significantly year over year, and we need more connective tissue to keep pace. We're hiring a Senior Manager, Business Operations to connect the commercial side of the house, so the teams that win, onboard, and retain our customers work from the same facts and toward the same goals. This role sits in Finance.

This is a people manager role. You'll lead our Revenue Operations (RevOps) team and act as an operating partner to senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, Customer Success, Implementation, People, and Finance. Together with the RevOps team, you own the shared language of the business (definitions, data, and processes) so commercial teams make decisions from one view of it.

When something important doesn't have an owner yet, it lands with you: a partnership strategy, a new commercial model, or pricing and margin guidance for leadership. This is a fast-moving startup. We're building toward a much larger company but we aren't one yet, and most of this role has no playbook. You'll define the process, build the tool, and hand it to the team. If you want established structure, this likely isn't the role.

You'll report to the Director of Business Operations and work directly with senior leaders across the company as a neutral partner to every function rather than an advocate for one. Your work will be visible, and it will significantly influence how we make commercial decisions.

You'll lead a small, high-ownership RevOps team that runs the systems and data our commercial teams depend on, and creates leverage for them to do their jobs even better. The team doesn't just own systems. They build automation, tooling, and processes, and they're close partners to Sales and Marketing. You'll coach them, set priorities, and open up more strategic scope, while giving them real autonomy.

Learn the business end to end: the revenue funnel, the customer lifecycle, how each commercial team works day to day, and where decisions get stuck

Build trust with your RevOps team: learn the work they own, and set priorities and team rhythms together

Deliver the first version of client-level margin and cost-to-serve, the analysis that grounds our pricing strategy

Develop a first point of view on pricing and packaging for revenue leadership, built on that analysis

Put AI to work in your own workflow from week one, and identify the first set of manual, repetitive processes across the commercial teams worth automating

Stand up a first version of a deal desk, including an approval workflow, pricing and discount guardrails, and standard commercial terms

Partner with Sales and Marketing leaders as they prepare for the monthly business review: pressure-test their plans and point out risks, then solve them with the RevOps team by creating new processes, tooling, or automation

Build on the strength of the RevOps team: prioritize its roadmap against business outcomes and take on more strategic work together

Launch your first AI-enabled workflows or automations that remove manual reporting or reconciliation for the commercial teams, with time saved tracked

Have a team whose output and standards hold, with the processes, tooling, and automation you've built running without you in the room

Have AI-enabled, self-serve tooling that commercial teams use day to day, with measurable time savings

Be the person leadership trusts to pick up the next important thing that doesn't have an owner yet

5-8 years of total experience that combines consulting or banking with time as an operator inside a company

Experience managing people: you've coached ICs, given direct feedback, made tough talent calls, and built team rhythms

Startup experience. You've worked in a fast-moving, ever-changing environment where structure wasn't already there, and you built it. This is not a role for someone whose experience is only at large, established enterprises.

Strong analytical and financial modeling skills, including forecasting. You're very comfortable building models in spreadsheets from scratch, and they hold up to scrutiny. You use AI to move faster and sharpen your work, and you have the foundational modeling knowledge to know when its output is wrong.

Data-first and self-sufficient. You open the dataset before you build the slide, you're always looking for more data to test an idea, and you're comfortable getting into raw data yourself. You use AI to analyze it faster and know enough to check its work.

Commercial acumen: you can read commercial terms, spot the problematic ones, and reason about pricing, discounting, margin, and deal structure

A clear communicator who can walk an executive through a messy problem and leave them with a decision, and who is just as effective working alongside an AE, a CSM, or an engineer

Comfort with ambiguity. When something isn't set up yet, your instinct is to build it, not to wait for it. You create structure, manage competing priorities, and pivot when things change.

Relentless follow-through: you close loops, and people stop checking on what you've said you'll do

A partner's temperament: you challenge constructively without taking sides, you surface problems early, and you resolve cross-team conflict without escalating it

Industry experience in fintech, financial services, lending, banking, or credit unions

Please note that this role may evolve as our business needs change, so we appreciate your flexibility and adaptability.

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

Unforgettable Off-Sites: Twice a year, bond with colleagues in exciting destinations, fostering teamwork and fresh ideas.

Paid Time Off: Enjoy flexible PTO days yearly for relaxation and rejuvenation.

Stock Options: Joining us means having a stake in our success, so you'll receive stock options as part of your compensation package.

Home Office Setup: Create your ideal workspace with a dedicated budget for home office essentials.

Work Trip Budget: Grow personally and professionally with a budget for work-related trips and co-working.

Health Coverage: Prioritize your well-being with comprehensive health and dental insurance plans.

Clutch is a revolutionary vertical SaaS company, proudly backed by Andreessen Horowitz (A16z), aimed at revolutionizing the way Credit Unions engage and change the lives of their members. As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt.  Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. Our mission extends beyond mere financial transactions; we strive to fundamentally enhance the way credit unions interact with their members. By integrating cutting-edge technologies and user-centric designs, we help credit unions provide seamless digital experiences that are on par with leading tech companies. This approach not only preserves but revitalizes the longstanding tradition of community and member-focused service inherent to credit unions.

Clutch is an equal opportunity employer. All applicants will be considered for employment without attention to race, color, religion, sex, sexual orientation, gender identity, national origin, veteran or disability status.

Please approach interviews without the use of AI tools—our goal is to get to know how you think, solve problems, and communicate. Once you're in the seat, bring on the prompts!

can clearly explain complex technical concepts to others

#7122. Employers want AI skills. Here’s how colleges are adding them without new hires.

Ask a college president what students need to graduate ready for work, and AI is now near the top of the list. More than a third of entry-level jobs now require AI skills, nearly triple the share six months earlier. Yet 63% of faculty say recent graduates aren't prepared to use AI in the workplace.

Most academic leaders agree AI belongs in the curriculum. The harder question is who will teach it, especially at small, tuition-dependent institutions.

AI adds hurdles most new programs don't face:

So many institutions stall, and students graduate without the skills employers are hiring for.

Rize Education helps colleges lower costs and grow enrollment through a shared program model, letting them launch high-demand, career-focused programs faster and for far less than building them alone. Its two AI programs have been adopted 94 times across the network of institutions.

With more than 1,800 students enrolled through these courses, Rize Education can finally answer the question provosts ask most: do students actually value an online AI course?

Rize surveys students at the middle and end of every course. Here’s what they said:

Students rate these courses as highly as others. 89% of Applied AI responses and 83% of AI Literacy responses rate the course as good as or better than other online courses students have taken. Against on-campus courses, the figures are 76% and 73%.

Limited AI experience didn’t hold students back. AI Literacy students rated their prior familiarity with AI just 3.9 out of 7, yet rated their confidence in finishing with a good grade 6.2 out of 7.

A well-run course shapes the experience. Students gave their highest marks to clear assignment instructions, reasonable grading and helpful instructors. As one AI Literacy student put it: "This course is challenging but all of the assignments work together to give a clear, functional foundational knowledge of AI that can immediately be put to use in professional and private settings."

For more than 1,300 students, an AI course was their first Rize-powered course, a sign these programs draw students in rather than just checking a box.

Employers are asking for AI skills, and students are rating these courses as highly as anything else they take. The full results break down what 1,800 students said about both programs.

#7118. Researchers and faculty sue NSF over axing of its social science unit

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The federal agency said it would close the unit in fiscal 2027, in what the lawsuit described as a unilateral decision that violated Administrative Procedure Act.

Since April, NSF has “stopped funding opportunities for basic SBE research,” reassigned almost all of SBE's staff and cut ties with scientists who were working with the directorate, according to Monday’s lawsuit.

President Donald Trump also fired every member of SBE’s governing board, who plaintiffs said had been kept in the dark about the agency’s future since he retook office almost two years ago.

Their lawsuit argued that NSF breached the APA by failing to consider the outcome of doing away with SBE — namely, that the agency would be unable to fulfill its mandate to “initiate and support basic scientific research and programs” in the social sciences.

Each year, SBE distributes some $247 million in grants for research into the social, behavioral and economic sciences — nearly two-thirds of all federal grants in these fields, according to NSF.

“Without that funding, hundreds of promising research projects directed to the pressing problems of our time — from the use and effects of artificial intelligence to child development in the age of social media — will never proceed,” the lawsuit said. Thousands of people also will lose their jobs, it said.

The plaintiffs also said NSF has given no official rationale for why it decided to extinguish the directorate.

In response to questions from The Atlantic, a White House spokesperson said in a May statement that the Trump administration “is committed to cementing America’s dominance in cutting-edge technologies of the future — innovation that is being driven by advancements in hard sciences, not in ideologically-driven ‘social sciences.’”

But the administration never said why it is labeling entire fields such as psychology and economics as “ideologically driven,” the plaintiffs said in their lawsuit Monday. And even if it were to explain, they argued, the rationale would directly conflict with the statutes that created NSF, “which repeatedly direct the Foundation to support the social sciences.”

All of this has occurred with a significant power vacuum at the top levels of NSF. The agency has been without a permanent leader for more than a year and a half, after its last director, Sethuraman Panchanathan, resigned in April 2025. It is also without a deputy director.

Both positions require a presidential appointment and Senate confirmation. Trump’s pick for NSF director, former U.S. Department of Health and Human Services Deputy Secretary Jim O'Neill, has not been given a confirmation hearing.

NSF Chief of Staff Brian Stone, a defendant in Monday’s lawsuit, is currently performing the duties of the agency’s director. The vast majority of NSF's significant cuts and the corresponding tumult occurred during Stone’s tenure. He is set to retire at the end of the year.

“NSF cannot shut down the government’s largest source of funding for an entire scientific sector without a Director and without consulting the body Congress created to make such Decisions,” Monday’s lawsuit said. “And it certainly cannot do so with no explanation.”

Alongside two named college instructors, the organizations suing NSF are the Federation of Associations in Behavioral and Brain Sciences, the Society of Experimental Social Psychology, and the Social & Affective Neuroscience Society. The plaintiffs are represented by the advocacy nonprofit Democracy Forward.

#7112. New Industry Alliance to Work Toward Common Security Model for AI Agents

Okta has announced a coalition bringing together major cloud, security, data and application vendors to create a shared architecture for securing AI agents across enterprise environments.

The 12 founding members of the new Blueprint Alliance are AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Okta, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler. GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen are participating as strategic advisors.

The effort expands an earlier Okta framework into an open, multi-vendor reference architecture intended to address a basic problem with enterprise AI agents: An agent can be created in one system, authenticated through another, access tools and data from several others, and be monitored by yet another security platform.

The Alliance is focused less on how agents are built and more on how organizations can govern them consistently across vendor boundaries.

The Blueprint Alliance architecture organizes agent security around four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond?

Those questions translate into several practical areas:

The reference architecture also covers development-related systems. Its whitepaper includes agent frameworks, code repositories, CI/CD environments, code assistants, tool registries, Model Context Protocol servers, and command-line tools among the components that may need to be discovered or governed.

That makes the initiative relevant to development teams deploying agents that interact with internal APIs, MCP tools, databases, SaaS applications, or other agents. The architecture calls for those connections and permissions to remain visible and controllable as agents move from development into production.

A major part of the Alliance's work will be testing how security information and controls can move between products from different vendors.

The initial architecture references several existing standards and protocols, including MCP for agent-to-tool interactions, the Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework for security telemetry, and the Shared Signals Framework and Continuous Access Evaluation Profile for exchanging risk information.

The goal is for one part of an enterprise stack to be able to detect a risk condition and pass that information to another control point that can act on it. Depending on the implementation, that could include revoking access or containing an agent whose behavior has changed.

The Alliance says members will build and test reference integrations and publish results as interoperability work progresses. Specific cross-vendor implementations and performance results were not documented in the launch announcement.

Founding member Lovable also published a developer-oriented explanation of its participation, describing organizations that may have internally built agents, agents imported through SaaS platforms and locally running agents that all need to be inventoried and governed under a common model.

The Alliance's whitepaper recommends that organizations begin with foundational capabilities such as unified logging, telemetry, and agent discovery rather than attempting to deploy every control at once.

The architecture is available for community use, and the Alliance says it will continue evolving as members test integrations and additional participants become involved. Timelines for individual vendor integrations have not been published.

For more information, check out the Okta announcement here.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

As an umbrella term, education technology encompasses everything from learning management systems and student information systems to productivity tools and AI platforms. More specific language clarifying the difference between ed tech and consumer-facing tools would allow state leaders to match the right evidence, expertise, and policy to the right questions.

Microsoft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

NVIDIA and 36 other industry partners are targeting agent security across multi-vendor clouds by combining open technologies for workload identity, agent controls, vulnerability scanning, and software supply-chain security.

→ Baca artikel asli

#7109. Boston University misses revenue projections by $42M

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The private institution will have to change “on a much broader scale” rather than relying on one-time cuts, its CFO said Monday.

BU has already gone through painful cuts as it has looked to shrink expenses. Last year, officials announced plans to lay off 120 staff members while eliminating another 120 vacancies. The cuts were an effort to shave 5% from the university’s budget during the previous fiscal year.

“Our prior cuts were effective in giving us time for thoughtful transformation,” Tirella said Monday.

But officials say those sorts of measures aren’t enough to fix today’s issues. Instead, BU is looking at making “better use” of its facilities, standardizing some operations and reducing duplicating functions, said Chris Sedore, BU’s chief transformation officer.

Manuel Cuevas-Trisán, BU’s senior vice president who oversees HR, suggested the university could upskill and promote employees as it tries to make the most of its resources. “Each vacancy can become an opportunity to think about how the work is done,” he said.

BU officials are also mulling the institution’s offerings. Sedore said some recently launched master’s programs are performing well while others face low enrollment.

The revenue miss for fiscal 2027 follows challenges from the previous year. Last October, BU President Melissa Gilliam and other senior leaders announced a $30 million budget shortfall amid missed revenue and lower-than-expected graduate student enrollment. Leaders at the time sought to address the gap with one-time savings, which Tirella said are no longer enough to address the university’s fiscal challenges.

Those efforts helped shore up the budget overall. With both tuition and grant revenue sliding, BU’s overall fiscal 2026 revenue fell 1.4% year over year to $2.7 billion, but it preserved an operating surplus, according to its fiscal latest financials. However, that net surplus plummeted by 91.6% year over year to $4 million.

Despite fiscal challenges, BU has planned salary increases for employees this fiscal year, as well as spending on deferred maintenance and technology, officials said.

Tirella said BU’s endowment and reserves are providing the university a stable financial foundation. But they cannot fully address operating budget challenges, given restrictions on most endowment funds and fixed spending limits to maintain the endowment’s health over the long run.

“Our balance sheet, including cash reserves and the endowment, cannot sustainably offset ongoing declines in operating margins,” she said.

#7106. AI Platform Behind 12,000 E-mail Breaches Dismantled

The platform moved quickly. Since launching in February, EvilTokens had been tied to more than 12,000 compromised inboxes across over 10,000 organizations worldwide, according to Microsoft. Its victims included businesses and institutions in financial services, healthcare, construction, real estate, wholesale distribution, and higher education.

Microsoft and its partners seized 50 websites used to operate EvilTokens and disabled more than 150 additional domains supporting the service. The Metropolitan Police Service in the United Kingdom also arrested two men on Sept. 11 in connection with the alleged operation.

At the center of EvilTokens was an AI chatbot designed specifically for cybercrime. Once attackers gained access to an inbox, the chatbot could analyze messages, map relationships, identify employees involved in payments, and recommend ways to impersonate trusted contacts.

Microsoft said the platform helped criminals determine "who to target, who to impersonate," and how to get the most money from a compromised organization.

Business e-mail compromise has traditionally required attackers to manually comb through messages, learn how an organization handles payments, and find the right person to impersonate. However, EvilTokens simplified the entire process by automating much of that work.

The tools could quickly sort through a stolen inbox, summarize or translate messages, find invoices and wire-transfer discussions, and identify the people who moved money. EvilTokens could then draft messages that sounded like they came from the victim. It packaged all of this with mailbox access, phishing tools, management dashboards, and even customer support.

The service was sold through Telegram for a $1,500 startup fee, followed by a $500 monthly subscription. Microsoft's complaint also described a bulk e-mail tool that cost $600 a month and a more advanced e-mail delivery platform priced at $1,000 a month.

To break into a target, EvilTokens relied on device-code phishing. Victims were sent to a real Microsoft sign-in page and persuaded to enter an authentication code. The login process looked legitimate because it was, but the code quietly authorized a session controlled by the attacker.

That also made cleanup more complicated. The attackers stole OAuth tokens and authenticated sessions instead of passwords, so a password reset alone might not remove their access. Administrators also needed to revoke the stolen tokens and active sessions.

According to Microsoft's complaint,  EvilTokens used fake Microsoft 365 security notices and other branded templates to direct users into the device authentication flow. Once inside, attackers could use Microsoft Graph and other APIs to search and monitor a mailbox.

The company also found that AI played a role in creating EvilTokens itself. Investigators said much of the platform appeared to have been "vibe coded," with its developers using AI to quickly build and expand the service. EvilTokens reportedly drew on multiple AI models, including improperly integrated services from Groq and OpenAI.

The court authorized Microsoft to take control of or disable domains connected to the operation. Microsoft worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, the Shadowserver Foundation, and TRM Labs on the disruption. Health-ISAC joined the case because healthcare organizations were among the targets.

This marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit's 40th court-authorized disruption and its first targeting an end-to-end, AI-enabled cybercrime service.

EvilTokens' infrastructure may be offline, but the model behind it is unlikely to disappear. The operation showed how AI can turn a stolen inbox into an immediately useful fraud playbook, allowing less-experienced criminals to launch attacks that once required considerable time and skill.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#7103. Alvernia University to acquire Marian University

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The two Catholic institutions, which are hundreds of miles apart, said the merger would be based on shared values rather than financial necessity.

Alvernia said neither university is entering the merger due to financial trouble.

“Both institutions are approaching this opportunity from a position of strength, guided by a shared commitment to mission alignment, long-term stewardship and expanded opportunities for students,” Alvernia said in its FAQ.

But when compared on financial strength, the two universities are in different weight classes.

Alvernia has posted multimillion budget surpluses over the past decade, outside of a reduced surplus in fiscal 2020 and a small deficit in fiscal 2024. In contrast, Marian’s finances have fluctuated more, and it has run a deficit since fiscal 2023.

The difference likely comes down to enrollment, as it does at most tuition-dependent colleges.

Alvernia had 2,796 students in fall 2024, a 2.4% increase from five years prior, according to federal data. Marian’s student body, meanwhile, declined 24.8% to 1,246 over the same period.

In 2023, Marian leadership declared an enrollment emergency and enacted a round of cuts, eliminating over 20 academic programs and laying off an undisclosed number of staff.

Marian Board Chair Holly Brenner said Monday that the merger with Alvernia will allow Marian to preserve its legacy and create new opportunities.

“Throughout this process, protecting Marian’s mission and identity has been at the heart of our discussions,” Brenner said in a statement. “We believe this proposed path forward with Alvernia offers an opportunity to build on Marian’s strong foundation, create new possibilities for our students, and carry our mission forward into the future.”

In turn, Michael Duff, chair of Alvernia’s board, called the acquisition “a thoughtful, mission-aligned step” for the university, one that “reflects Alvernia’s strength, its disciplined stewardship and its commitment to pursuing opportunities that are consistent with our mission.”

The proposed merger is pending regulatory and accreditor approval, as well as formal sign-off from the universities’ boards and affiliated religious orders. Both institutions said they will seek input from faculty and campus groups as details are hammered out.

#7100. Penn State wrestles with $124M budget hole

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The public institution projects a $87 million year-over-year drop in tuition revenue amid a fall enrollment decline.

Just over 70% of Penn State’s budget hole is due to falling tuition and fee revenue.

Behind the revenue miss is a fall headcount that is 3,058 students short of the comparable 2025 number. Breaking that down, Penn State said Monday that it enrolled 742 fewer graduate students, 922 fewer international students and 2,384 fewer out-of-state students across its campuses. All three of those groups can have an outsized impact on tuition revenue at a college.

About half of the enrollment decline came at seven regional campuses that are set to close — and had been expected. In explaining the declines, the university also pointed to nationwide trends, including widespread decreases in graduate enrollment and international students.

At the same time, Penn State logged its largest-ever undergraduate class of 43,010 students and a record class of 9,907 first-time, full-time students.

“Even with these challenges, Penn State's market position is strong,” said Matt Melvin, Penn State vice president for enrollment management, in a statement.

Health insurance costs made up the remainder of the university’s fiscal 2027 shortfall in its $2.2 billion education and general budget. By August, healthcare claims had grown 15.7% year over year, the university said Monday. Budgetwise, that led to a $37 million jump above projections.

On an FAQ page about its budget, Penn State said it had no plans for broad cuts to its programs or student services, or for changes in its hiring processes, as it looks to tighten spending. The institution also said it would hold tuition and fee prices steady despite the revenue dip.

The university does, however, already have plans to nix some 47 undergraduate programs after a multiyear review of its offerings. Collectively, those programs enroll about 1.1% of Penn State’s undergraduates. The institution listed another 50 programs as “strategic growth opportunities.”

Penn State’s plan to close seven of its 19 regional Commonwealth campuses is primarily an effort to shore up the institution’s budget. Keeping those locations open would cost the university $40 million annually in financial support and overhead expenses and require a $200 million investment in facilities, according to a 2025 report recommending the closures.

#7098. Redesigned Microsoft Copilot Will Build Apps and Work on Its Own

Micorosft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need, and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

The new Copilot experience has three main parts: Home, Code, and Autopilot. It also brings Word, Excel, and PowerPoint into the Copilot app. For users, the pitch is simple: Start a task in one place and stay there as it grows from a quick question into a document, an app, or a longer-running project. Most of the new capabilities are still rolling out or in preview.

Home puts Chat and Copilot Cowork together. Chat remains the place for quick answers, research, and drafts. Cowork handles work a user wants to delegate, such as preparing a briefing or pulling together a response to a request for proposals. Microsoft said Copilot eventually will route requests to Chat, Cowork, or Code on its own.

Office in Copilot will allow users to create a Word document, Excel workbook, or PowerPoint deck without leaving that experience. The files remain editable in the regular Office apps, and colleagues can work on them at the same time. Someone building a presentation, for example, could draw on a workbook, draft the slides, and keep refining both files from within Copilot.

Code is aimed at people who need a tool but might never open a development environment. A user can describe a tracker, dashboard, app, or workflow in plain language and have Copilot build it. Microsoft calls these "small, purpose-built solutions." The feature uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot and runs in a sandboxed environment. A separate Copilot Managed Runtime, now in preview, lets organizations host and share the resulting apps within Microsoft 365.

Then there is Autopilot, previously known as Scout. It is designed for work that does not end with a single response: following a discussion, checking in with stakeholders, or keeping a project moving over several days. "Give it a name, a role, and a goal," Microsoft said. Autopilot runs in the cloud and has its own identity, memory, and workspace in the customer's tenant. Microsoft said its actions are subject to permissions, auditing, and organizational controls.

Those longer-running tasks also change how customers pay for Copilot. In a separate pricing post, Microsoft said everyday work in Chat and Microsoft 365 apps will remain covered by a per-user subscription. Cowork, Code, and Autopilot will be billed separately through usage-based Copilot Credits. A Copilot user subscription will still be needed to access those features.

For enterprise customers, the features that charge by usage will stay off until an admin sets up a spending policy in the Microsoft 365 admin center. Microsoft said there will be no charges before then. Admins can set budgets for the whole organization or for specific groups, limit how much individual users can spend, and get alerts when costs approach a set amount. Reports will show who is using the features and which tasks are driving costs.

Microsoft is bringing Code and Copilot Managed Runtime under those cost controls alongside Cowork. Copilot Studio agents are expected to follow in October. Before turning the features on for more employees, IT departments will need to decide who can use them, how much they can spend and which tasks are worth the extra cost.

Home and Code will begin rolling out through Microsoft's Frontier early access program in the coming weeks. Code's Frontier rollout starts at the end of September, when Autopilot also expands to private preview.

For more information, visit the Microsoft site.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

As an umbrella term, education technology encompasses everything from learning management systems and student information systems to productivity tools and AI platforms. More specific language clarifying the difference between ed tech and consumer-facing tools would allow state leaders to match the right evidence, expertise, and policy to the right questions.

#7096. Baylor stares down $30M deficit after undergraduate enrollment dip

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Baptist institution in Texas is pausing hiring and delaying capital spending as it tries to shore up the budget gap.

Livingstone’s deficit announcement follows a slide in Baylor’s undergraduate enrollment this fall.

Baylor’s total undergraduate headcount fell 2.3% year over year to 13,860 students, according to institutional data. First-time, first-year enrollment dipped 6%, representing a decline of over 200 students, which could bring ongoing financial ramifications given that they might have continued paying tuition for several years.

“Undergraduate enrollment is realigning to historic levels now that the two large COVID-era freshman classes have matriculated through the University,” Livingstone said. The university brought record numbers of students in 2020 and 2021, bucking the national trend during that period.

However, graduate enrollment increased by 3.8% to 5,888 students.

Livingstone rattled off a long list of headwinds that have dogged Baylor’s budget. Among them were rising spending, families wary of college prices, competitors increasingly discounting tuition to recruit students and “increasing volatility in forecasting budgets in an uncertain financial environment.”

Baylor’s leader also acknowledged the sum the university owes the head football coach it just fired after a slow start to the season. The buyout reportedly amounts to an estimated $12 million for Dave Aranda. Livingstone described the costs as a one-time expense not included in the deficit and “a liability of Baylor Athletics that will be addressed solely by the department.”

In the previous fiscal year, the Division I university brought in about $63 million in revenue from college sports, according to its financial statements.

Baylor started the calendar year in cost-cutting mode. In February, Livingstone announced a plan to shave $35 million from the university’s budget over two years, which she framed as necessary to limit tuition increases on students. Among other moves, the university slashed retirement contributions for employees.

In her message Monday, the university president acknowledged the sacrifices Baylor’s workforce has already made to help shore up the budget.

“As faithful Christian stewards, we must work together to prepare Baylor to succeed in a very uncertain future for colleges and universities that is likely to look quite different than the past,” she said.

Baylor joins a handful of others to issue early reports on fiscal 2027 woes amid enrollment pressures.

Just this week, Boston University leaders said the private institution missed revenue estimates by $42 million so far, and Pennsylvania State University revealed a projected $124 million budget hole due primarily to tuition revenue declines. Both institutions plan on budget cuts but did not signal that layoffs would be part of the savings efforts.

#7092. Colleges report declines in international enrollment amid federal restrictions

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Survey data released Wednesday found that nearly 6 in 10 U.S. colleges lost foreign graduate students this fall, and 45% saw a decline in undergraduates.

Wednesday’s survey, released by NAFSA: Association of International Educators in partnership with the Oxford University Press and the student recruitment platform Studyportals, reinforces concerns that the Trump administration's attacks on foreign students and the long-standing U.S. programs that support their study here would corrode the international enrollment pipeline.

Survey respondents cited the administration’s crackdown on student visas and the astronomical new fees sought for the H-1B visa and Optional Practical Training programs. While some of those policies are being challenged in the courts, they can signal to international students that they are unwelcome in the U.S., and the uncertainty and instability can ward off prospective applicants.

Major public research universities, traditionally popular among foreign students, have begun reporting international enrollment declines this fall. And some, like Pennsylvania State University, have drawn a direct line between that loss of students and budgetary challenges.

“These survey results affirm what we’ve observed before: national policies matter to international students making major decisions about where to study, conduct research, or start a career,” Fanta Aw, NAFSA’s executive director and CEO, said in a statement Wednesday.

Some colleges also reported decreased interest in countries that have historically sent the most higher education students overseas, such as China and India.

“Our agents in India in particular are telling us that they are seeing a drastic decline in inquiries for US graduate study and that many prospective students are either considering other destinations or postponing study plans,” one university respondent said.

Of those surveyed, 37% of U.S. colleges said they planned to diversify the locations where they recruit from and tap into new markets over the next 12 months.

One institution said it would pivot to the Philippines, Canada and Colombia as a means of offsetting losses from India, Bangladesh and Nepal, the three countries from which the college traditionally got the most foreign students.

Another 32% said they would expand their online offerings, and 30% said they would turn to budget cuts.

However, cuts could further exacerbate challenges for colleges’ international admissions offices. One respondent admitted that staffing issues have “resulted in a lack of attention to incoming students.”

Pollsters surveyed colleges’ senior administrators and international student recruitment professionals online between Oct. 5 and 26.

#7090. AI Leaders Warn of Risks at United Nations Security Council Briefing

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York on September 23 to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

They did not offer a single plan for managing those risks. Bengio called for licensing and independent safety reviews. OpenAI's Sam Altman proposed common standards that countries could adopt through their own laws. Anthropic's Dario Amodei urged AI developers to slow down when safety work cannot keep pace. Hugging Face's Clément Delangue warned that limiting access to capable systems could leave defenders at a disadvantage.

France, which held the Security Council's rotating presidency, convened the meeting on AI and international security. The discussion exposed a difficult question for governments: how to oversee technology that crosses borders when countries disagree about who should set the rules.

Bengio, co-chair of the UN's Independent International Scientific Panel on AI, challenged the idea that competition forces companies to keep building more powerful systems. "The race is not a law of nature: it is the product of choices," he said in his published remarks. "They've told us they would slow down if they could. They can."

He called on developers to show independent experts that frontier systems are safe to train and deploy. He also proposed incident reporting, licensing, and liability insurance. Bengio identified catastrophic misuse, concentrated power, and the loss of human control as risks that require international cooperation.

Altman acknowledged the danger of moving too quickly. "We have unilaterally slowed down in the past. We will do so in the future," he said, according to remarks published by OpenAI. Decisions about AI's direction "cannot be made by labs in San Francisco alone," he added.

Altman proposed complementary national and international standards to measure frontier AI capabilities, assess risks, and test safeguards. He said countries should be able to compare evidence and verify commitments. He also called for faster reporting of serious incidents and secure channels for sharing emerging threats. Such standards should accommodate both open and closed models without favoring established companies, he said.

Amodei made a more direct commitment about the pace of Anthropic's releases: "We will slow down as much as necessary," he told the council. He identified two principal concerns: the misuse of AI to develop biological weapons and the possibility that advanced systems could outstrip developers' ability to control them.

The Anthropic chief proposed placing outside evaluators inside AI companies with access comparable to employees. He suggested that governments begin with a narrow agreement against using AI to make biological weapons, then develop ways to evaluate models and verify commitments across borders. He also called for common testing standards and a system for notifying other countries of significant AI incidents.

Delangue drew on a July incident in which Hugging Face disclosed an AI agent cyberattack. He called for stronger monitoring and disclosure, but argued that safety also depends on giving defenders access to capable tools. The central danger, he said, was "asymmetry of powerful AI" between attackers and defenders, large companies and everyone else, and wealthy countries and the rest of the world. His team used an open-source model while responding to the attack, he said.

The U.S. government opposed a broader international regulatory role. White House science adviser Michael Kratsios told the council that countries should develop their own ability to use and govern the technology. In the U.S. statement, he rejected new global governance structures and used the phrase "superintelligence, formerly known as AI," adopting President Donald Trump's terminology from his General Assembly speech the previous day.

The four AI briefers differed on how far oversight should go and how widely capable systems should be available. They agreed on a narrower point: Governments need better ways to understand what advanced AI systems can do and to detect when safeguards fail.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#7086. York College nears $7.4M purchase of shuttering Penn State campus

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The private institution intends to turn the 53-acre property into a healthcare education hub through a partnership with a local hospital system.

While Penn State saw the York campus as a financial burden, York College said Thursday it sees the location as an opportunity.

York College President Thomas Burns said in a statement that the private college “is uniquely equipped to preserve this campus as a central educational hub.”

Early planning between York College and WellSpan call for an expansion of nursing and other healthcare education partnerships. Those partnerships include one struck last year between WellSpan and Temple University’s medical school to open a regional campus in York.

York College said it would finance the property acquisition with its endowment — last valued at $193.8 million — while WellSpan would commit “significant financial investment” toward capital improvements on the campus. According to Penn State, the campus had a nearly $30 million maintenance backlog as of last year.

Penn State will continue operating the campus through the spring 2027 semester as previously announced. Afterward, pending approval of the sale, York College will “lead campus operations” while WellSpan will have an “operational and instructional” presence on the campus.

Penn State opted last year to close its York campus as part of a plan to shore up its central budget and refocus on its strongest areas.

At Penn State York, enrollment declined by over 40% to 703 students between 2014 and 2024, according to a university report. Enrollment declined by 15% between 2019 and 2024.

“Despite the efforts of campus leadership and faculty to stabilize enrollment through program enhancements and community outreach, the numbers have not rebounded,” Penn State administrators wrote.

Meanwhile at York College, enrollment slid 15.1% to 3,656 students between 2019 and 2022, according to federal data. But over the next two years that increased to 3,825 students. And the college says it has seen four consecutive years of increases.

In recommending the York campus closure, Penn State administrators cited the ongoing enrollment slide, as well as “regional market saturation” and “high operating costs relative to its size and scale.”

On the last point, they pointed to challenges related to scale. Its small campus, which has 15 buildings but no student housing, made it difficult to attract students outside the commuter region.

“While its facilities are functional, the campus lacks the infrastructure or strategic location necessary to serve as a long-term hub,” the leaders wrote in their 2025 report. “As enrollment has fallen, per-student operating costs have increased, and the campus has become increasingly dependent on central resources to remain operational.”

As for regional competition, York College is just a mile away, as is a community college. Penn State’s own Harrisburg campus is 17 miles away, presenting internal competition. Altogether, there were 19 colleges within 30 miles of Penn State York, according to the university’s report.

Prior to York College’s offer, local officials had asked Penn State to donate the campus for a local arts and workforce training center.

Renata Engel, a Penn State vice president who oversees the commonwealth campuses, said in a statement Thursday, “The community planning process identified continued educational use as a priority, and this proposal would place the campus with an established local institution while also serving the University’s financial interests.”

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Designed in partnership with the National Science Teaching Association, the standards-aligned "Carnival of Collisions: Contact Forces" and "Mission MagLev: Electric & Magentic Forces" courses offer free video lessons, guided activities, and supplemental teaching resources for grades 6-8.

Class CrunchLabs was launched last year to offer free hands-on science curriculum resources including teaching guides, classroom slides, student readings and handouts, assessments, and instructional videos featuring Rober alongside "Science Bob" Pflugfelder, celebrity guests, and student co-hosts. The initiative was backed by a $7.2 million grant to create the CrunchLabs.org Foundation, a nonprofit "dedicated to revolutionizing science education in a way that promotes critical thinking and inspires lasting curiosity and confidence in STEM," according to a news announcement at the time.

The organization plans to roll out additional courses on YouTube over time, with the ultimate goal of posting more than 1,000 science videos in 34 languages.

#7080. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

As an umbrella term, education technology encompasses everything from learning management systems and student information systems to productivity tools and AI platforms. More specific language clarifying the difference between ed tech and consumer-facing tools would allow state leaders to match the right evidence, expertise, and policy to the right questions.

Microsoft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

NVIDIA and 36 other industry partners are targeting agent security across multi-vendor clouds by combining open technologies for workload identity, agent controls, vulnerability scanning, and software supply-chain security.

#7077. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. #7075. Trump taps DEI opponent to lead DOJ’s civil rights division

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While at the U.S. Department of Education, Craig Trainor threatened to strip federal funding from colleges over their diversity initiatives.

Trainor’s selection signals that the Trump administration isn’t backing away from its heavy crackdown on colleges and their diversity initiatives. Trainor is set to replace Harmeet Dhillon, one of several top DOJ officials who stepped down after Democrats seized control of both chambers of Congress in the midterm elections.

Under Dhillon, the department’s civil rights division aggressively investigated colleges over pro-Palestinian protests and their policies for admissions, leading several high-profile colleges to cut deals with the Trump administration to end those probes.

The DOJ has even sued the University of California system, alleging that its Los Angeles campus hasn’t adequately responded to harassment toward Jewish and Israeli students.

The agency also sued Harvard University in February to force the Ivy League institution to hand over extensive admissions data and again in March to cut off its access to federal student aid. Judges dismissed both cases against Harvard, but the agency has since appealed the federal student aid case to the 1st U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals.

DOJ officials have recently stepped up their crusade against initiatives aimed at recruiting a diverse study body. So far, its investigations into admissions policies have led the department to accuse 10 medical and law schools of racially discriminating against White and Asian applicants.

Trainor has likewise spearheaded major efforts to stamp out DEI in the higher education sector. While serving as the Education Department’s acting assistant secretary for civil rights, Trainor threatened in February 2025 guidance to pull federal funding from colleges over DEI initiatives ranging from race-based scholarships to affinity graduation ceremonies.

Federal courts have since struck down the guidance. However, the DOJ released similar sweeping guidance last year that threatens to strip federal grant funding from colleges over what the agency deems as illegal DEI. DOJ officials flagged several commonplace practices as potentially unlawful, ranging from requiring job applicants to provide diversity statements to having identity-based lounges — even if they are open to all.

Trainor pursued similar anti-DEI policies in his most recent appointment as the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development’s assistant secretary for fair housing and equal opportunity. In that role, Trainor issued guidance warning colleges that affinity housing may violate federal law.

#7074. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

As an umbrella term, education technology encompasses everything from learning management systems and student information systems to productivity tools and AI platforms. More specific language clarifying the difference between ed tech and consumer-facing tools would allow state leaders to match the right evidence, expertise, and policy to the right questions.

Microsoft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

NVIDIA and 36 other industry partners are targeting agent security across multi-vendor clouds by combining open technologies for workload identity, agent controls, vulnerability scanning, and software supply-chain security.

#7071. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#7066. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].
